### Preface

—humanity, however, having been so weakened and corrupted because of their disobedience and sins that they could never again reach the end intended for them by God through their own strength; nevertheless, the gracious, kind, and merciful Heavenly Father has always shown His fatherly help and grace toward them. Indeed, He has even pointed out special means through which one may not only be strengthened and raised up again, but also very easily, and without special toil and labor, attain one’s desired end and <term>salvation</term> <note>The original German "Seligkait" implies both eternal happiness and the state of being saved</note>.

Among these means, the <term>Spiritual Estate</term> <note>The "Geistlich Stand" refers to the formal religious life of monks, nuns, and clergy</note> and the God-devoted <term>cloistered life</term> <note>Life lived within a monastery or convent under religious vows</note> is, as it were, the most eminent. It contains within itself so many benefits and useful things that one could only with great effort enumerate them all. For, to mention now but a few of these: this blessed estate works so powerfully within those people who surrender themselves to it with their whole heart and will, that they not only flee and avoid all manner of vice and sin, but even keep themselves clear from all opportunities for them. And on the other hand, all—

<summary>Newmayr continues his preface, explaining that although sin weakened humanity, God provided the "Spiritual Estate" (monastic life) as a powerful means to avoid vice and easily attain salvation.</summary>

<keywords>original sin, grace, spiritual estate, monastic life, salvation, vice, repentance</keywords>

### Preface

—the times and their course abundantly demonstrate and testify. Indeed, the envious Devil, through this tyranny and persecution—his own and that of his instruments—which he has unceasingly practiced and still practices against the pious Christian <term>cloister folk</term> <note>Original: "Closter leutten." This refers to those living under religious vows, such as monks and nuns</note>, seeks nothing other than the eradication and destruction of the true Christian Catholic Religion. 

For he perceives and feels what great damage the members of religious orders inflict upon his kingdom. They stand particularly in his way and—through their godly meditation, with prayer, fasting, vigils, the <term>mortification of their flesh</term> <note>Original: "Casteyung jres Fleischs," the practice of self-discipline or physical penance to overcome sin</note>, and even through reading, writing, preaching, exhortations, and all kinds of God-pleasing Christian works—they hinder and repel his devilish intent and purpose. 

If these people were cleared out of the way, he would then find it easy to lead humanity into the greatest <term>barbarism</term> <note>Original: "barbariam," a Latin loanword</note> and savagery, and even into the most terrifying error and paganism, as unfortunately already appears to be the case in many places and regions throughout the wide world.

<meta>catchword: Now</meta> <note>The original German catchword "Nuhn" (Now) indicates the first word of the following page.</note>

<summary>Newmayr argues that the Devil specifically targets religious orders because their spiritual disciplines—such as prayer, fasting, and scholarly work—are the strongest defenses against his influence. He warns that without these "cloister folk," the world would fall into paganism and barbarism.</summary>

<keywords>spiritual warfare, monasticism, Catholic Church, asceticism, religious orders, barbarism, Counter-Reformation</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---

### Preface

Now, however, as the cursed Satan has ever and always raged and stormed against the aforementioned <term>most holy state</term> <note>Original: "Hochheyligen Stand," referring to the religious or monastic life</note>—both by himself and in his own person, as well as through his members and followers—so, on the other hand, Almighty and Merciful God has never allowed it to perish entirely, but has kept a constant seed of it. 

And thus, from the time of the Holy Apostles until now, He has at all times awakened certain prominent holy men who have preserved this blessed state. They have not only protected and defended it with words and deeds, as well as with their spiritual writings, but also with diligence and in all kinds of ways and manners have planted and advanced it. Indeed, they have primarily and most diligently instructed and taught—and partly also recorded with the pen—how and in what manner a truly Christian <term>religious person</term> <note>Original: "Ordens Person," a person belonging to a religious order</note> should primarily be constituted, and what they should do or refrain from. 

Such special, prominent, and fervent zealots of the holy, aforementioned spiritual state and cloistered life have specifically been <term>St. Basil the Great</term> <note>original: "H. Basilius magnus," a 4th-century bishop and theologian who was influential in the development of communal monasticism</note>, <term>Jerome</term> <note>original: "Hieronymus," an early church father known for his translation of the Bible and his promotion of the ascetic life</note>—

<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: —rome</meta>

<summary>Newmayr explains that throughout history, God has raised up holy men like St. Basil and St. Jerome to defend and define the monastic life against the attacks of the Devil. These leaders provided both the spiritual defense and the practical rules for how a member of a religious order should live.</summary>

<keywords>St. Basil the Great, St. Jerome, religious orders, monasticism, spiritual defense, church fathers, providence</keywords>

Whosoever then also counts among these zealous founders and protectors of orders the Blessed, highly learned, and spiritual man Father *Thomas Malleolus* <note>The name "Malleolus" is a Latinization of his family name, "Hamerken," meaning "little hammer."</note>—who is otherwise called Thomas à Kempis because of his fatherland and the city where he was born into this world—will, in my opinion, not go very far wrong:

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Thomas à Kempis, following his introduction on the previous page as a figure of spiritual authority comparable to the great saints and founders of religious orders.</meta>

### Preface

For although he founded no new Order, yet after he had devoted himself to the liberal arts in his flourishing youth for seven years in Deventer among the spiritual and devout *Brothers* <note>original: "Fratribus"</note> of the Order of Saint Jerome, and had *progressed* <note>original: "proficiert"</note> very well, and thereafter entered the Order of Saint Augustine—specifically joining the *Canons Regular* <note>original: "Canonicis regularibus," a community of priests living under a rule</note>—he led such a godly, edifying, and holy life in such a *religious state* <note>original: "Religion"; here meaning the monastic life or rule</note> and Order for 71 years (since his entire life extended into his 90th year) that he may well be considered blessed. Not only by his godly conduct and life, but also by his devout, spiritual writings, he gave cause and encouragement to many toward similar godliness and a true Christian monastic life. His magnificent, precious, indeed golden *little works* <note>original: "Opuscula"</note> which he left behind, and which are now found here and there, demonstrate what a zealous, spiritual, and godly man he was.

<sig>A 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: Dann</meta>

<summary>This page describes the life of Thomas à Kempis, noting his education in Deventer and his 71 years of holy service as an Augustinian Canon Regular, emphasizing how his life and "golden" writings inspired others to Christian devotion.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas à Kempis, Deventer, Brethren of the Common Life, Order of St. Jerome, Augustinian Canons Regular, monastic life, Opuscula</keywords>

### Preface

For who can sufficiently praise his noble little books, which he wrote on the *Imitation of Christ* <note>original: "Nachuolgung Christi"</note> and on the Holy Sacrament of the most holy *Body of Christ* <note>original: "Fronleichnambs Christi," referring to the Eucharist</note>? Who can enough extol his *Soliloquies of the Soul* <note>original: "Soliloquia animæ"</note>, that is, the conversation of the soul, and his heartfelt exhortations and letters which he composed for the *novices* <note>original: "Nouitios," those in their probationary period in a monastery</note> and other spiritual brothers and members of religious orders? Who can also sufficiently praise (besides many others of his very prayerful, spiritual, and entirely useful little books) the instructive *Spiritual Alphabet for Monastic People*, and the *Little Book of Consolation for the Poor and Sick*, together with the *Manual* <note>original: "Enchiridion"</note> and *Life of True Monks and Religious Persons*, as well as the *Little Handbook for the Humble*, and especially the *Five Useful Little Books* which he wrote on *monastic discipline* <note>original: "Closterzucht"</note>? All of these are found arranged in order right at the beginning of the second volume <note>original: "Tomi"</note> of his *collected minor works* <note>original: "Opusculorum"</note>. Truly, for those in religious life—of whatever status or gender they may be—these are pure golden book-

### Alphabet

<margin>B</margin>
<meta>Catchword: others</meta>

the way which you desire to walk: I will also look upon you specially with my gracious eyes. But at the beginning, pay diligent attention to the Alphabet, which I set down for you here.

**A**ttend to and seek not the praise of men, nor to be preferred above others; but much more: to be despised by them. For that will be much more wholesome and useful for your eternal life than if you were praised and lauded by the multitude.

**B**e diligent to do good to all people, to the wicked as well as the pious, and see to it that you are neither superior nor burdensome to anyone.

**C**apture <note>original: "Captiuier"</note> <term>Capture (from the Latin *captivare*), meaning to restrain or keep under control</term> and guard your heart against all wandering, and also your tongue and mouth against useless words and speech. Keep also your

# A Spiritual [Book/Alphabet]
<meta>catchword: adversity-</meta>

other senses in constant, earnest discipline and watchfulness.

# Consolation of the Poor

So too, you, my limping and lame brother, rejoice that you are now despised by many. Because you cannot run through the streets and alleys with the mighty, the strong, and the rich—who enjoy themselves in various sensual pleasures <term>sensual pleasures: "Wollustbarkaitten," referring to physical or worldly delights that lead one away from spiritual focus</term> of the flesh and soon thereafter descend into Hell—stay now in solitude and silence. Pray unceasingly for yourself and for others, and especially praise Christ that he has taken so many opportunities for sinning away from you. He has shown and pointed out to you the gates of the Kingdom of Heaven, to be entered by the narrow way <note>A reference to Matthew 7:14: "Because strait is the gate, and narrow is the way, which leadeth unto life."</note>.

Likewise, you too should rejoice, humble and silent Brother Columbanus <note>Likely referring to Saint Columbanus (c. 540–615), an Irish missionary known for his strict monastic rule that emphasized silence and humility.</note>, you who are without deceit and without gall <term>without gall: an idiom meaning without bitterness, resentment, or malice</term>; be now—

<summary>This page encourages the physically disabled to see their limitations as a spiritual protection against worldly sin, while introducing Brother Columbanus as a model of quiet, guileless humility.</summary>

## Of the Purity and Sublimity of the Holy Apostle John. The 3rd Chapter:

"Behold, this is my chosen Son, whom I have chosen for myself." <note>While these words are typically associated with God the Father speaking of Jesus, the author applies the theme of divine election to John’s special status.</note> What has gifted the Holy Apostle John with such a high and clear **understanding** <term>understanding: "Verstandt," referring here to spiritual insight and the intellectual capacity to grasp divine mysteries.</term>, and caused him to be loved more than others by our Lord **JESUS Christ**? And how is it that he understood and wrote of such high—indeed, even higher—things of God than all the **Doctors** <term>Doctors: "Doctores," referring to the highly learned theologians and scholars of the Church.</term> in the whole world? This was caused by the fact that he was adorned with an especially great **purity** <note>original: "Rainigkeit" / "Reinigkeit"</note> of his heart and his body; and also that he possessed such a special, perfect, and fervent **love** toward God and his neighbor, which he [expressed] not only with

<meta>catchword: words</meta>

Likewise, as it pleases God in heaven, so let His divine will be done; I wish for nothing else, I desire nothing else, as the reward for all my labor in heaven, than You alone, O my heart and my God.

## On the Obedience of a Humble Subject toward his Prelate and Superior. The 4th Chapter.

It is fitting for a pious subject <term>Vnderthon: in this monastic context, a monk or subordinate within the religious order</term> to accept the commands of obedience with a joyful heart, and to retain nothing at all of his own will. Instead, following the example of Christ <note>original: "Exempel Christi"</note>, he should willingly submit himself to the hand of God and to his superior <term>Obristen: the highest authority or head of a religious house, such as an Abbot or Prior</term>; for that is the most pleasing gift that can be offered

<meta>catchword: who [German: wer]</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a reflection on giving thanks to God as the source of all good and begins the fourth chapter, which defines monastic obedience as the total surrender of one's will to a superior, modeled after Christ.</summary>

<keywords>monastic discipline, obedience, humility, prelate, religious superior, will of God, imitation of Christ, spiritual sacrifice</keywords>

# The First Book
## 17

...to be. Therefore, every subject <term>Underthan: a monk or subordinate within the religious order</term> should guard himself against passing insolent judgment <note>original: "freuentliche vrthaile"</note> upon his superior <term>Obern: a higher-ranking official</term> and leader <term>Vorſteher: an overseer or prior</term>. Nor should he pry into his superior’s actions and omissions with meddling curiosity <note>original: "fürwitzigklich"</note>; rather, he should interpret and construe his words and deeds in the best possible light.

And even if certain things should appear in him from which little benefit is expected—yes, even if some flaws and shortcomings were to be found in him—a subject should still not betray him or judge him. Instead, he should excuse him and bear with him in kindness. When it is appropriate, he should also kindly admonish him, or else, either by himself or through another as an understanding person, beg and remind him in a completely filial <note>original: "Kindtlichen"; implying the humble, respectful love of a child for a parent</note> manner, of what a great burden <note>The catchword "Burd" (Burden) indicates the text continues on the next page to describe the weight of the superior's responsibilities.</note>

<summary>This page advises religious subjects to avoid judging their superiors' flaws and instead to interpret their actions charitably and offer respectful, filial corrections when necessary.</summary>

# How a monastic person should especially learn to love their cell and the solitary life.
## The 7th Chapter.

Whoever loves their cell <note>original: "Cellen"; referring to the small, private room of a monk or nun, which serves as a sanctuary for prayer and study.</note> and gladly dwells within it will be protected from many sins and temptations <note>original: "anfechtungen"; a term frequently used in this period to describe spiritual trials, inner conflicts, or the "assaults" of the devil on the soul.</note>. Indeed, the more diligently they inhabit it, the more they please God and their superiors <term>Superiors: translated from "Obrigkeit," referring to the administrative and spiritual authorities of the monastery, such as an Abbot or Abbess.</term>, and will be loved by them all the more. However, the less one attends to it and commits themselves to being there, the more they will feel a sense of dread and resentment <note>original: "grausen vnd verdruß"; this refers to the spiritual state of acedia, or "spiritual sloth," where a monk feels a deep weariness and desire to flee their religious duties.</note> toward it. It is well for the person who loves and gladly dwells within it, for the fragrant ointments and graces of the Holy Spirit will instruct such a person in all good things. Blessed is he to whom the grace is given...

# Of the Choir / and how one should conduct oneself in the performance of the Divine Offices.
## The 8th Chapter:

The choir <note>original: "Chor"; refers to the designated area in a church or monastery where the clergy or monks gather for communal prayer and the liturgy.</note> is a place and location which is hallowed and consecrated to God and the holy angels: In this place, the Divine Office <note>original: "Göttlich ampt"; the structured cycle of daily prayers and services performed by religious orders.</note> is carried out in the presence of the servants of God, with special reverence and devotion, as well as with the singing of psalms. And the clergy <note>original: "Geistlichen"; literally "spiritual ones," referring to monks, priests, or those in religious orders.</note> are arranged in the choir just like the angels in heaven. The work and duty of the dear holy angels is that they should praise God at all times: Likewise, it is also the duty of the clergy that they sing psalms with an attentive mind <note>original: "Gemüeth"; refers to the inner soul, spirit, or disposition of the heart.</note> and pray with devotion. # The First Book

What does it profit you that you praise God for one little hour <note>original: "Stündlein"</note>, and consume the other with worldly and vain things? You should not hold the precious benefit of your devout prayer and godly works in such low regard <note>original: "schnöd achten"</note> that you alternate them with trivial reading, jesting, and foolish chatter <term>narrentädungen: literally "fool's talk," referring to idle, nonsensical, or frivolous conversation that distracts from spiritual focus</term>. For devotion <term>Andacht: a state of inward religious feeling and prayerful focus</term>, which is not kept in check by silence, very soon goes to ruin. 

When you feel a weariness from a long prayer, then consider within yourself that after it has an end, you will ascend up into Heaven. And if this has no effect on you, then consider that it is much easier to stay awake, pray, and sing for three or four hours, than to burn for just one single hour in Purgatory <term>Fegfewr: in Catholic theology, a place of temporary suffering and purification before entering Heaven</term>. # Chapter 8: That it is much better to obey God than friends. <note>original: "Freundten." In this period, "friends" often referred specifically to family members and kinsmen.</note>

## The Old Father.

Therefore Christ, the eternal Truth, said to His disciples who followed Him: "Whoever loves father and mother more than me is not worthy of me." <note>A reference to Matthew 10:37, a cornerstone text for the monastic tradition justifying the "holy departure" from the family home.</note> For this reason, one should not listen to those who advise staying in the world for the sake of the comfort of kinsmen; instead, one should much more follow those who, for the sake of the love of Christ, have chosen— <meta>The text ends mid-word "er-" with the catchword "wölt," completing the word "erwölt" (chosen) on the following page.</meta>

My dear son, from your youth onward, strive to take up instruction; thus you will not lack wisdom even into your old age. <note>original: "Mein lieber Sohn von deiner Jugent an befleiß dich anzunemben die Lehr..." # Of the Good Manners of a
# Humble Brother / and conversely
## of the Vices of a Proud
## and Lazy Brother.
### The 10th Chapter:

**God has regarded the prayer of the humble / and has not despised their supplication** (Psalm 101). <note>original: "Psal: 101." In the Vulgate numbering used at the time, this refers to what is now Psalm 102:17: "He will respond to the prayer of the destitute; he will not despise their plea."</note> This is a heartfelt teaching / which teaches us to pray humbly / and not to despair of God's mercy. Therefore, O Lord, look upon my humility / my nothingness / look upon my weakness / and also my daily failings / and for the sake of your holy Name / and through the merits of your dear Saints in Heaven / and of the pious people upon the earth / forgive me all my sins. ## Of a Lazy Brother.

A sluggish and lazy brother / is, as it were, always sick <note>The author suggests laziness is a form of spiritual or feigned illness.</note> / he is commonly in all places and ends / and at all times the last / he is also completely devoted to idleness and sleep: He always arrives very late to work / and leaves early from it: He is inclined toward strolling / but slow to return home: He rejoices at eating / and is sorrowful at fasting: He is quick to sleep / but lazy at rising: He is hoarse for singing <note>Likely referring to the singing of psalms or liturgy in choir.</note> / but ready for chattering: He does not like to hear of obedience / and always limps toward the

Work: A lukewarm heart brings the soul heavy spiritual ailments and a distaste for divine works, and it seeks outward comforts that offer little benefit. This is best overcome through good works, holy readings <note>original: "h. Lectionen"; refers to the practice of *Lectio Divina*, a traditional monastic practice of scriptural reading, meditation, and prayer.</note>, and constant prayer, along with the remembrance of Christ's bitter Passion. Whoever wishes to overcome vice must often do violence to his own nature <note>This "violence" refers to the disciplined suppression of physical impulses or "the flesh" in favor of spiritual growth.</note>, humbly calling upon the grace of Christ and waiting for it with patience.
